| Event segmentation at boundaries | **Well supported** | 48 |
| The doorway effect specifically | **Conditional.** More dependent on memory load than early reports suggested; at least one replication failed under low load **[SVR]** | 48 |
| Ceiling height changes cognitive style (Meyers-Levy & Zhu 2007) | **E4.** Social priming; replication status not verified by this book **[SVR]** | 49 |
| Dark/light adaptation asymmetry | **Basic visual physiology** | 50 |
| Uncertainty raises arousal | **Well supported** | 50 |
| Place cells, grid cells, head-direction cells | **Solid**; Nobel Prize 2014 | 51 |
| Hippocampal involvement in episodic memory; Maguire on taxi drivers | **Solid** | 51 |
| Method of loci; Dresler et al. (2017) training study | **Supported** | 51 |
| Awe: vastness + need for accommodation; prosocial correlates | **E3–E4.** Young literature, modest effects, some small samples and photo/video inductions | 51 |
| Persinger's "God helmet" | **FAILED REPLICATION.** Granqvist et al. (2005), double-blind; suggestibility predicted the reports, not the field | 51 |
| Newberg/d'Aquili neuroimaging of contemplatives | **E4–E5.** Small samples; large inferential leap; routinely overinterpreted | 51 |
| A brain region for religious experience | **No such finding exists** | 51 |
| Situational dependence of apparent character traits | **Well supported**, and it cuts against the architectural model of character | 45 |
| Self-report of *why* an experience mattered | **Unreliable** — old and replicated | 45 |
| Voluntary association membership: social and wellbeing correlates | **Broadly favourable**, and silent on whether content matters beyond belonging | 45 |

### The rule behind the register

The claim "the building shapes your thinking" is, in its experimental form, a
social priming claim — and social priming is the area of psychology most damaged by
the replication crisis of the 2010s.

Two findings in this table would have strengthened this book's argument
considerably had they been reported in their strong form. Both are reported weak.

---

# APPENDIX R
## Source Verification Register

Every point at which this book could not verify a source to its own standard,
collected in one place. A reader who wants to attack the book's findings should
start here.

| # | Ch. | What is flagged | What would close it |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 5 | Precise citation for an excavation report relied on for comparative Levantine architecture | The published report, checked directly |
| 2 | 9 | Martin Metzger, *Königsthron und Gottesthron* (1985) — German, cited at page level from secondary discussion | The German text, consulted directly |
| 3 | 11 | The precise scope of the Talmudic argument at Eruvin 14a relative to the rim/bore explanation of the molten sea | The primary text, in context |
| 4 | 22 | A systematic collation of early English and continental vernacular renderings of 2 Chronicles 2:13. The general pattern of transliteration is well attested; the per-edition detail is not verified here | Edition-by-edition collation |
| 5 | 26 | Specific edition and pagination for Scholem's essay on the Star of David | The edition consulted |
| 6 | 48 | Publication details of the doorway-effect replication attempt described, cited from memory | The paper |
| 7 | 49 | Replication status of Meyers-Levy & Zhu (2007) on ceiling height | A current literature search |
| 8 | 59 | Lévi's lodge, the date of his initiation, and the circumstances of his departure — given differently in different sources | Lodge records or a documented account |
| 9 | 66 | Whether Leon Templo's reconstruction specifically influenced Masonic Temple imagery, as distinct from the shared biblical source or Villalpando's engravings | A Masonic document showing a feature derived from Leon specifically |
| 10 | 76 | The earliest datable description of the pigpen grid system, and the route by which it reached eighteenth-century Masonic use | A dated source establishing priority |
| 11 | 79 | Any assertion of L'Enfant's Masonic membership. **The burden lies with whoever makes it**; this book has found nothing | A membership record |
| 12 | App. F | Eruvin 14a, as above | — |
| 13 | App. G | Architect attributions and dates in the Masonic buildings survey, compiled from secondary reference descriptions | Primary architectural records |

### How to read this register

**A flag is not a concession that the claim is wrong.** It records that the book's
own verification standard was not met at that point, and says what would meet it.

**Nothing load-bearing depends on a flagged item.** The findings in Appendix E do
not rest on any entry above. Entries 6 and 7 support a caution rather than a
claim — they are flags on findings the book *declined* to lean on. Entry 11 is a
flag on somebody else's assertion.

**The register exists because the alternative is worse.** A book with no visible
gaps is either exhaustively verified or concealing something, and the second is far
more common than the first.

---

# APPENDIX S
## What Would Change These Findings

A finding that nothing could overturn is not a finding. This appendix states, for
each of the book's principal negative conclusions, the evidence that would change
it.

### On the Temple's construction and design

**"The Temple's dimensions encode no hidden ratio."**
Would change with: a text from the relevant period stating a proportional
intention; the ratio appearing across many independent buildings of the tradition
at a rate above the control rate; φ approximations systematically tighter than
chance allows; or a single match precise to a fraction of a per cent.

**"The magical-construction claim is unsupported."**
Would change with: any source contemporary with or close to the construction
connecting it to magical means, or a demonstrated transmission channel from the
period to the *Testament of Solomon*.

### On Masonic origins

**"Freemasonry did not inherit Temple knowledge."**
Would change with: a craft document earlier than the eighteenth century containing
material demonstrably derived from Temple sources beyond the shared biblical text —
a dimension, a doctrine, or a name that could only have come from a non-biblical
Temple tradition.

**"The Kabbalistic reading of the pillars is post-1854."**
Would change with: an eighteenth-century Masonic source stating it. One document
would do.

**"The Hiramic legend does not descend from an operative tradition preserving the
name."**
Would change with: a pre-eighteenth-century craft document giving the master mason
a stable name matching later usage, or a systematic collation showing the Old
Charges' name variation to be narrower than Chapter 32 reports.

### On the psychological claims

**"Initiatory institutions' moral effects are untested (E4)."**
Would change with: a study using baseline measures, a comparison group of people
who wanted to join and did not, long follow-up, and defensible outcome measures.
This is difficult but not impossible, and nobody has done it.

**"Architecture does not induce specific inner states."**
Would change with: well-powered, pre-registered, directly replicated experiments
showing that specific architectural properties produce specific cognitive effects.
The awe literature is the most likely place for this to happen.

### On the conspiracy claims

**All six E6 ratings in Appendix O.**
Would change with: primary documentation. A minute, a directive, a fund, a policy,
a correspondence. The absence of such documentation after two centuries of
assertion is the finding; a single authenticated document would be a different
finding.

### On this book's method

**The controls.** If a reader runs the published scripts with different but
defensible parameters and gets materially different results, the conclusions drawn
from them change. The scripts are short and the parameters are visible, which is
the point of publishing them.

### What would not change these findings

Repetition. Confidence. The age of a claim. The prominence of the person making it.
The fact that a great many people believe it. The absence of evidence being
interpreted as evidence of suppression — which Chapter 78 identified as the
mechanism by which a framework becomes unfalsifiable, and which this book does not
accept from anyone, including itself.

---

# APPENDIX T
## Glossary

**Ashlar.** A squared building stone. A technical term of the trade before it was a
symbol. The rough ashlar is undressed, the perfect ashlar dressed and true (chs
43–44).

**Atbash.** A Hebrew letter substitution exchanging the first letter for the last,
the second for the second-to-last, and so on. Attested within the Hebrew Bible
(ch 74).

**Availability is not transmission.** That a text or idea existed and could in
principle have been read establishes possibility, not influence. The recurring
distinction in Parts XI–XII.

**Concinnitas.** Alberti's term for the condition in which nothing can be added,
taken away, or altered without harm (ch 56).

**Disagreement principle.** When sources agree on a role but disagree widely on the
details attached to it, the role was transmitted and the details were not (chs 32,
40). Carried from the preceding volumes.

**Emplacement.** Jonathan Z. Smith's thesis that ritual works substantially by
declaring where it is, making ordinary actions significant by location (chs 34,
36).

**Event segmentation.** The parsing of continuous experience into discrete events,
with boundaries making prior information less accessible (ch 48).

**Forking paths.** Andrew Gelman's term for the undeclared analytic freedom that
gives a single analysis the error rate of many (ch 72).

**Half-of-everything rule.** A feature with very low cardinality — a shape anyone
can draw, a direction with two options — carries almost no information about
descent (chs 5, 26, 36, 39). Carried from the preceding volumes.

**Hekhalot / Merkavah.** Late-antique Jewish visionary literature of ascent through
heavenly palaces to the divine throne-chariot (ch 52).

**Old Charges.** The manuscript tradition of English craft documents, c. 1400–1700s,
containing an invocation, a legendary history, and the charges of the trade
(ch 32, App. I).

**Parallel is not descent.** Two traditions sharing a feature is not evidence that
either learned it from the other. The book's most frequently applied rule.

**Precommitment.** Fixing the target, the rule, and the tolerance before the search.
The single most important methodological practice in this subject (chs 72, 73).

**Retrofitting.** Discovering a held system inside an object that does not contain
it, by exercising undeclared analytic freedom. Requires no bad faith (ch 72).

**Rule of direction.** A source is primary for its own period only (App. B).

**Sefirot.** The ten emanations of Kabbalistic cosmology, arranged in the structure
known as the Tree of Life.

**Shamir.** In rabbinic narrative, the agent capable of cutting stone without iron,
obtained by Solomon from the demon Ashmedai — a story that exists to explain 1
Kings 6:7 (ch 28).

**Status Quo.** The arrangement governing religious sites in Jerusalem, deriving
from Ottoman decrees of the 1850s and carried forward, with modifications, by
successive authorities (ch 63).

**Symbolic universe.** A frame in which other symbols are located, rather than a
symbol among others. Recognisable by its capacity to absorb new material without
being altered (ch 34).

**Tracing board.** A painted board carrying the emblems of a degree, set up in the
lodge. A development of the earlier practice of drawing the lodge on the floor and
erasing it (ch 35).

**Zecher l'churban.** The custom of leaving a portion of a house unfinished in
remembrance of the Temple's destruction (ch 85).

# APPARATUS

# ILLUSTRATION LIST

Seventy-eight plates are proposed, within the brief's range of sixty to ninety.
The full plan, with holdings, dates, and chapter placements, is in
`illustrations/illustration_plan.md`. This list states the principles and
summarizes the sections.

## Principles

**No image is invented.** Every item is either a real, locatable object — a
manuscript, an excavation plan, a building, a printed plate, a photograph — or an
original diagram declared as such.

**Reconstructions are labelled as reconstructions.** Chapter 66 established why
this matters: a drawing removes the uncertainty a text contains, and a reader who
sees only the drawing cannot tell which parts were given and which supplied. Every
Temple reconstruction plate carries its artist, its date, and a note of which
textual source it is built on.

**Comparative material is labelled with its own provenance**, not as illustrating
the Temple.

**[SVR]** marks items whose shelfmark, accession number, or museum attribution
requires confirmation before captioning.

## Sections

| Section | Content | Plates |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Textual and archaeological: Masoretic pages, Tel Dan Stele, 'Ain Dara and Tel Motza plans, Megiddo, City of David, topographic maps, Phoenician comparanda | 9 |
| 2 | Temple Mount and Jerusalem: platform imagery, Dome of the Rock, Warren's survey, Herodian masonry, the Trumpeting Place and warning inscriptions | 10 |
| 3 | Reconstructions: Nicholas of Lyra, Arias Montano, Villalpando's plates, Leon Templo, Schott's model, Newton's plan, modern comparative reconstructions | 14 |
| 4 | Imperial and comparative: Arch of Titus relief, Gudea cylinders, Near Eastern temple plans, the Escorial | 8 |
| 5 | Craft documents: Regius and Cooke manuscript pages, Old Charges rolls, Schaw Statutes, Edinburgh Register House MS, Anderson 1723 and 1738 title pages and plates, Prichard 1730 | 12 |
| 6 | Masonic material culture: tracing boards from floor-cloth to Harris, aprons, lodge interiors, the buildings of Appendix G | 13 |
| 7 | Esoteric: Rosicrucian vault imagery, Lévi's Baphomet (1856), Golden Dawn materials, the Waite–Smith High Priestess (1909) | 7 |
| 8 | **Original diagrams**, declared as such | 5 |

## The five original diagrams

1. **The five-level model** (Appendix A) — building, text, symbol, ritual setting,
   inner Temple, with the direction of movement and its datable transitions.
2. **The four-source pillar discrepancy** (Appendix F) — 1 Kings, 2 Kings, 2
   Chronicles, and Jeremiah drawn to their own stated measurements, side by side.
3. **The golden-ratio control distribution** (Chapter 73, Appendix M) — the
   synthetic null's hit-count histogram with the Temple's single hit marked on it.
4. **The hexagram-in-a-lattice control** (Chapter 79) — the jittered street grid
   with the best-fit hexagram drawn on it, at map scale.
5. **Lodge orientation against Temple orientation** (Chapter 36) — the two plans
   with their sacred poles marked, showing the reversal.

Diagrams 3 and 4 are outputs of the published scripts and are reproducible by any
reader who runs them.
